The Club Sandwich: Gapless Phases and Phase Transitions with Non-Invertible Symmetries
Lakshya Bhardwaj, Lea E. Bottini, Daniel Pajer, Sakura Schäfer-Nameki

Abstract
We provide a generalization of the Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT) framework to characterize phase transitions and gapless phases with categorical symmetries. The central tool is the club sandwich, which extends the SymTFT setup to include an interface between two topological orders: there is a symmetry boundary, which is gapped, and a physical boundary that may be gapless, but in addition, there is also a gapped interface in the middle. The club sandwich generalizes so-called Kennedy-Tasaki (KT) transformations. Building on the results in Bhardwaj et al. 2024a ; Bhardwaj et al. 2025 on gapped phases with categorical symmetries, we construct gapless theories describing phase transitions with non-invertible symmetries by applying suitable KT transformations on known phase transitions provided by the critical Ising model and the 3-state Potts model. We also describe in detail the order parameters in these gapless theories characterizing the phase transitions, which are generally mixtures of conventional and string-type order parameters mixed together by the action of categorical symmetries. Additionally, removing the physical boundary from the club sandwiches results in club quiches, which characterize all possible gapped boundary phases with (possibly non-invertible) symmetries that can arise on the boundary of a bulk gapped phase. We also provide a mathematical characterization of gapped boundary phases with symmetries as pivotal tensor functors whose targets are pivotal multi-fusion categories.

–
is to find concrete physical implications tied to the presence of these symmetries. Such “smoking gun” applications will not only substantiate the importance of these symmetries, but take their relevance from mere curiosities to genuinely fundamental concepts underlying the inner workings of quantum field theories (QFTs). The goal of this paper is to find such applications in the realm of phase transitions in the presence of categorical symmetries, and provide a characterization of the possible gapless phases at these critical points. This has applications in both high-energy physics and condensed matter alike, and should provide a unified framework to characterize phases in the presence of categorical symmetries in any dimension.

Characterizing phase transitions for standard group-like symmetries is of course well-understood and relies on a simple organizing principle: that of spontaneous symmetry breaking, going back to Landau’s work. Many short-comings of this principle are known by now, but most pertinently it fails when the symmetry of the system is non-invertible or more generally categorical.

In this paper we build on the earlier works
Bhardwaj et al. 2024a
;
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
, where we proposed a framework to characterize gapped phases with non-invertible symmetries, and study phase transitions between such gapped phases and provide a characterization of the gapless phases at the critical points.
The classification of gapped phases is most succinctly formulated using the so-called Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT) or Symmetry Topological Order
Ji and Wen 2020
;
Gaiotto and Kulp 2021
;
Apruzzi et al. 2023b
;
Freed et al. 2022
, often referred to as the “sandwich”: a
d+1
dimensional TQFT sandwiched between two
d
-dimensional boundaries.
We will provide a brief summary of the construction shortly. The SymTFT allows essentially the separation of symmetries and QFTs/phases, and most importantly contains the information about the generalized charges under non-invertible symmetries
Bhardwaj and Schafer-Nameki 2023
.
Gapped phases are characterized by inserting gapped boundary conditions both for the symmetry boundary and the physical boundary – the latter ensures that the resulting
d
-dimensional theory is topological, the former realizes the categorical symmetry. The generalized charges, encoded as topological operators of the SymTFT, become order parameters for the gapped phases
Bhardwaj et al. 2024a
;
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
, see also
Kong et al. 2020b
;
Kong et al. 2020a
;
Chatterjee et al. 2022
;
Kong and Zheng 2022
;
Kong et al. 2022
and in 2d
Lan et al. 2015
;
Ai et al. 2016
;
Cirac et al. 2021
;
Chen et al. 2011
;
Garre-Rubio et al. 2023
;
Chen et al. 2013
;
Duivenvoorden and Quella 2013
;
Quella 2020
;
Quella 2021
;
Xu and Schuch 2021
;
Xu et al. 2022
.

To characterize phase transitions between gapped phases with categorical symmetries, we will need to extend this picture to include another layer to the sandwich, i.e. we will promote it to a
club-sandwich

1
1

1

A club sandwich is comprised of three slides of bread containing two distinct fillings.
: two topological orders in
d+1
dimensions, separated by a
d
-dimensional interface, and book-ended on either side with
d
-dimensional boundary conditions.

The topological orders can for instance be SymTFTs
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
and
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
for two symmetries
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, in which case we call
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
a reduced topological order

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{d}
=
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
{\mathcal{S}}

(I.1)

Here
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the symmetry boundary of the SymTFT for
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
the interface. The physical boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{d}
is a not-necessarily gapped boundary condition of the SymTFT for
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, and thus in the standard sandwich construction would describe an
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
-symmetric theory after interval compactification. The key point here is that the collapse of the club sandwich yields a theory that is
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric. This map from
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
-symmetric theories to
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric theories may be thought of as non-invertible duality transformations
Li et al. 2023a
;
Li et al. 2023b
generalizing the so-called Kennedy-Tasaki (KT) transformation
Kennedy and Tasaki 1992a
;
Kennedy and Tasaki 1992b
.

However, the topological orders do not necessarily have to be thought of as SymTFTs and this framework can be viewed more generally as a way to study
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric boundary conditions of a topological order
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
(now not viewed as the SymTFT for another symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
).

We will be interested in phase transitions between two
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases, which are characterized in terms of two gapped boundary conditions of
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. This in turn corresponds mathematically to so-called Lagrangian algebras in the center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. The interfaces
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
of interest are characterized by non-maximal (and thus non-Lagrangian) condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. In the process we can also characterize all the order parameters, which are manifestly encoded in the SymTFT and the club sandwich generalization.

Recent works that use SymTFT techniques to extend the study of gapless phases and phase-transitions – though applied to group-like symmetries – are
Chatterjee and Wen 2023
;
Huang and Cheng 2023
;
Fechisin et al. 2025
;
Wen and Potter 2025
.
The setup in
Chatterjee and Wen 2023
is precisely the case of the club-sandwich, when the topological orders are both SymTFTs and the interface is the intersection of two Lagrangian algebras, applied in the context of group symmetries
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}
in (1+1)d.
More precisely:
a phase transition between two gapped phases, which are defined by the Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
respectively, is characterized by
{\mathcal{A}}_{1,2}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
which simply means the set of topological lines that are both in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
, provided this maximal common subalgebra is condensable.

Higher order phase transitions can occur when three or more such gapped phases meet. As
{\mathcal{A}}_{1,2}
is not maximal, it will lead to a reduced topological order, as described via what we call a
club-quiche
.
However, not all condensable (non-Lagrangian) algebras are of this type and we will see examples with different setups, not necessarily group-like.

I.1
Summary of Setup

Standard SymTFT Construction.

We begin with a theory
\mathfrak{T}
in
d
spacetime dimensions, with a fusion
(d-1)
-category symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
. The first concept that we can associate to this is the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
, which gauges the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
in
d+1
dimensions. The topological defects of this SymTFT form the so-called Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
of
{\mathcal{S}}
.
The symmetry itself is realized as topological defects localized along a gapped boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
of the SymTFT. The
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetric theory
\mathfrak{T}
is then recovered as the interval compactification of
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
upon selecting another boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}
on the right. This is the so-called “sandwich” construction, which we schematically depict as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}):=

(I.2)

It is useful to also consider the ‘‘quiche”
2
2

2

A quiche is most certainly not an open sandwich but we will retain this terminology introduced in
Freed et al. 2022
.
associated to the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
, namely the combination of the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
and the gapped boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
(but without the physical boundary condition), which we display as

\mathfrak{Q}_{d}=\left(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\right)=
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}

(I.3)

If
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is also a gapped boundary condition of
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
, then the interval compactification leads to a gapped
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric phase
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
;
Bhardwaj et al. 2024a
. Such
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases will be denoted by

\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}=\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}}),\mathfrak{B}^{\text{top}})\,,

(I.4)

where
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{top}}
is another topological boundary condition of
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
.
Gapped boundary conditions of
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
are classified by Lagrangian algebras in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. In (1+1)d these can be systematically classified for a given SymTFT.

Note also that two symmetries
{\mathcal{S}}
and
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}
related by (possibly generalized) gaugings have the same SymTFT, but correspond to different symmetry boundaries of that SymTFT.

Club Sandwich and Quiche.

In this paper we extend this set-up to allow for gapless phases with
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry.

The first step is to generalize the construction of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases to one for
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundaries. This requires the introduction of what we call a “
club-quiche
” (i.e. an open club sandwich). Concretly, this is realized by coupling a
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
via a topological interface
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
to the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
with topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
. This club quiche will be depicted by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}^{C}(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}}),{\mathcal{I}},\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}):=

(I.5)

\displaystyle\hbox to216.31pt{\vbox to66.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.09416pt\lower 16.47183pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{170.78421pt}{64.86978pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

and realizes an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{d}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
upon collapsing the interval between
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
.

In practice, in the cases of interest, the interface
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
is specified by a
condensable
algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
(generically non-Lagrangian) in
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
, and therefore we can express
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
as a reduced topological order for a symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}

\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}=\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})/{\mathcal{A}}=\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})\,.

(I.6)

The symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
is generically smaller than (or equal to, in the case of trivial
{\mathcal{A}}
)
{\mathcal{S}}
. See appendix
B
for necessary conditions to determine condensable algebras. We note that our approach examines only necessary, but not sufficient, conditions for an algebra to be condensable. Specifically, our treatment is at the level of objects, following, for instance
Chatterjee and Wen 2023
, without explicitly verifying the possible multiplication structures of the algebras. This presents a limitation, as a given algebra may admit multiple multiplication structures, potentially leading to more club-quiches than the ones we consider. Nevertheless, we believe that the algebras we study are fully consistent, as they yield a well-defined reduced topological order. Furthermore, in a forthcoming work
Gai et al. 2025
a subset of the authors compute the algebra multiplication for all group-theoretical cases and confirm that no additional algebras arise.

We can complete the club quiche to a
club-sandwich
by slotting in a physical boundary condition onto the right hand side of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
. This results in a theory
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
with symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{d}
=
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
{\mathcal{S}}

(I.7)

One may ask now how this is different from the construction of the standard SymTFT (
I.2
).

Phase Transitions.

One central application is that of phase transitions and construction of gapless phases with
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry. Say we start with a phase transition for a symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, e.g.
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which in 2d is modeled by the Ising CFT. We can then construct using the KT transformations from
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
to the larger symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
provided by the club-quiche a phase transition that is
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric, i.e. a gapless phase that is
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric.

I.2
Overview of Results

In sections
II
and
III
we summarize the construction of the SymTFT quiches and sandwiches, respectively. We then extend this to the club quiches in section
IV
, where we sketch the general
d
-dimensional construction, and give explicit examples for
d=2
(i.e. 3d topological orders separated by the topological interface
{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
), for abelian and non-abelian groups, non-invertible symmetries such as
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
,
\mathsf{Ising}
and more general Tambara-Yamagami
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
fusion categories. The club quiches are completed to club sandwiches in section
IV
, which provide a KT transformation between two symmetry categories
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
. Finally, in section
VI
we explain how this can be applied to construct phase transitions with categorical symmetries
{\mathcal{S}}
.
We provide examples using input phase transitions for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(Ising) and
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
(3-state Potts model) to construct new phase transitions.
In appendix
A
we provide a mathematical perspective on the club sandwich construction.

We summarized all the examples and the relevant sections where they are discussed in table
1
.

II
SymTFT Quiche

As defined in
Freed et al. 2022
, a quiche
\mathcal{Q}
is a pair

\mathcal{Q}_{d}=(\mathfrak{B}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1})

(II.1)

of a
(d+1)
-dimensional
3
3

3

We always display full spacetime dimensions.
(oriented, unitary) TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
and a
d
-dimensional topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}_{d}
of
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
. We will sometimes call such a quiche as a
d
-dimensional quiche for convenience, though it should be kept in mind that a
d
-dimensional quiche has a
d
-dimensional component, and a
(d+1)
-dimensional component. We display a quiche as

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
\mathfrak{B}_{d}

(II.2)

Our focus will mainly be on
d=2
in this work. An irreducible
d
-dimensional quiche is a quiche that cannot be expressed as a sum of other
d
-dimensional quiches. Practically, this means that the space of topological local operators of
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
is one-dimensional (comprising of scalar multiples of the identity operator in the bulk), and the space of topological local operators of
\mathfrak{B}_{d}
is also one-dimensional (comprising of scalar multiples of the identity operator on the boundary).

II.1
Gapped Boundary Phases

Physically, quiches are important objects to study for understanding gapped boundary phases, which we now define.

First we review the definition of gapped (bulk) phases. A
d
-dimensional gapped phase is defined as an equivalence class of
d
-dimensional gapped systems, in which two gapped systems are regarded to be equivalent if one of the systems can be deformed into the other without closing the gap (even at infinite volume). One can identify a bosonic gapped
d
-dimensional phase
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}]
as a deformation class of
d
-dimensional TQFTs
4
4

4

Here, and in what follows, we assume that we are working with systems having emergent Lorentz symmetry in the infrared.
. An irreducible gapped phase is defined to be a gapped phase that cannot be expressed as a sum of other gapped phases. In other words, an irreducible gapped phase has a single vacuum (at infinite volume)
5
5

5

Vacua can also be understood as ground states of the system when the space is taken to be a sphere. Another characterization is in terms of topological local operators the system admits.
. Practically, the space of topological local operators of a TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d}
lying in an irreducible gapped phase
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}]
is one-dimensional (comprising of scalar multiples of the identity operator), or in other words such a TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d}
is an irreducible TQFT.

Consider now gapped systems that are comprised of a
(d+1)
-dimensional bulk and a
d
-dimensional boundary. A priori, a gapped bulk may have a gapless boundary, but we require here the boundary to be gapped as well. We define a
d
-dimensional gapped boundary phase
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]
to be an equivalence class of gapped bulk+boundary systems, in which two systems are regarded to be equivalent if and only if one of them can be deformed into the other without closing the gap in the bulk or on the boundary (even when both bulk and boundary have infinite volume). A couple of comments are in order:

•

Note that a
d
-dimensional gapped boundary phase
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]
comes associated to a
(d+1)
-dimensional gapped bulk phase
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}]
, which is the gapped phase the bulk system is in, away from the boundary. We will denote this by

[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]\mapsto[\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}]\,.

(II.3)

However, there may be multiple different
d
-dimensional gapped boundary phases
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]_{i}
, for which the
(d+1)
-dimensional gapped bulk phase is the same as
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}]

[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]_{i}\mapsto[\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}],\qquad\forall~i\,.

(II.4)

Thus, the presence of a boundary may add additional components to the phase diagram.

•

Gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phases
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]
whose associated gapped
(d+1)
-dimensional bulk phase
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}]
is trivial are the same as gapped
d
-dimensional phases discussed at the beginning of this subsection.

One can identify a gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phase
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]
as a deformation class of
d
-dimensional quiches

[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]=[(\mathfrak{B}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1})]\,.

(II.5)

An irreducible gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phase is one that cannot expressed as a sum of other gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phases. Practically, any
d
-dimensional quiche
\mathcal{Q}_{d}
lying in an irreducible gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phase
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]
is an irreducible quiche, as defined above.

Let us restrict to
d=2
from this point on. In this case, an irreducible gapped boundary phase comprises of a pair

[\mathcal{Q}]=([\mathfrak{B}],\mathfrak{Z})

(II.6)

where
\mathfrak{Z}
is an irreducible 3d TQFT and
[\mathfrak{B}]
is a deformation class of irreducible topological boundary conditions of
\mathfrak{Z}
. Such a 3d TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}
is described by the data of its topological line operators (also referred to as anyons), which form a (unitary) modular tensor category (MTC)
\mathcal{Z}
. This cannot be an arbitrary MTC as it must admit a topological boundary condition, which becomes the requirement that it must be possible to express the MTC
\mathcal{Z}
as the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
of a (unitary) fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

We can characterize a deformation class of irreducible topological boundary conditions of
\mathfrak{Z}
by a Lagrangian algebra in the MTC
\mathcal{Z}
. We refer to the deformation class corresponding to a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}
as
[\mathfrak{B}](\mathcal{L})
. The deformation class comprises of a (real) one-parameter family of irreducible topological boundary conditions

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(II.7)

These boundaries are related as

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L})=\mathfrak{T}_{\lambda}\boxtimes\mathfrak{B}_{0}(\mathcal{L})

(II.8)

i.e. the boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L})
can be obtained from the boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{0}(\mathcal{L})
by stacking it with an invertible 2d TQFT
\mathfrak{T}_{\lambda}
, known as the Euler term
6
6

6

Its partition function on a closed 2d manifold
\Sigma_{g}
(of genus
g
) is
Z[\mathfrak{T}_{\lambda},\Sigma_{g}]=\text{exp}\left(-\lambda\chi(\Sigma_{g})\right)
, where
\chi(\Sigma_{g})=2-2g
is the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma_{g}
.
. More generally, we have

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{2}}(\mathcal{L})=\mathfrak{T}_{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{1}}(\mathcal{L})\,.

(II.9)

The Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}
can be expressed as

\mathcal{L}=\bigoplus_{a}n_{a}\mathbf{Q}_{a}

(II.10)

where the sum is over the simple bulk anyons
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
in
\mathcal{Z}
. Physically, the presence of a term
n_{a}\mathbf{Q}_{a}
in
\mathcal{L}
means that there is an
n_{a}
-dimensional vector space of topological local operators along any corresponding topological boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L})
at which the line
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
can end

\mathfrak{Z}
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L})
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
n_{a}
dimensional

(II.11)

As an example, let the gapped bulk phase
\mathfrak{Z}
be the Toric Code, which is described by the well-known modular tensor category

\mathcal{Z}=\{1,e,m,f\}

(II.12)

where
e
and
m
are electric and magnetic bosonic lines, and
f
is a dyonic fermionic line. There are two (1+1)d gapped boundary phases with associated (2+1)d gapped bulk phase being the toric code. One of them corresponds to the Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}_{e}=1\oplus e

(II.13)

in which the electric line (anyon) can end along the boundary, and the other is described by Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}_{m}=1\oplus m

(II.14)

in which the magnetic line (anyon) can end along the boundary.

II.2
Symmetry TFT

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a symmetry that can arise in
d
-dimensional systems. The class of symmetries that we are interested in are described by (spherical) fusion
(d-1)
-categories. This includes as special cases finite symmetry groups with/without ’t Hooft anomalies and finite higher-form and higher-group symmetries with/without ’t Hooft anomalies. However, there are also more general fusion
(d-1)
-categories that are not of this type. Such categorical symmetries are also referred to as non-invertible symmetries, as they typically include symmetry elements that do not admit an inverse.

Given such a symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
, we can associate to it a
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
, known as the Symmetry TFT (or SymTFT). The symmetry TFT has the property that it admits at least one (but usually multiple) irreducible topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
such that the topological defects living on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
form the
(d-1)
-category
{\mathcal{S}}
. Thus, the setup of SymTFT involves quiches of the form

\mathcal{Q}_{d}=\left(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})\right)

(II.15)

which we display as

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}

(II.16)

Such a boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is known as a symmetry boundary of the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
.

Conversely, any irreducible quiche
\mathcal{Q}_{d}=(\mathfrak{B}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1})
provides an example of a SymTFT setup, with the symmetry being

{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{Q}_{d})={\mathcal{C}}_{\mathfrak{B}_{d}}

(II.17)

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathfrak{B}_{d}}
is the fusion
(d-1)
-category formed by topological defects living on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{d}
.

For
d=2
, the MTC associated to the 3d SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
can be identified with the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
associated to the fusion
(d-1)
-category
{\mathcal{S}}
. There is a canonical Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
that describes a symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}

\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}=\bigoplus_{a}n^{\text{sym}}_{a}\mathbf{Q}_{a},\qquad n_{a}^{\text{sym}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

(II.18)

where
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
are simple bulk anyons and
n_{a}
is the dimension of morphism space
\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}}(F(\mathbf{Q}_{a}),1)
in
{\mathcal{S}}
between the object
F(\mathbf{Q}_{a})\in{\mathcal{S}}
obtained by applying the forgetful functor
F:~\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\to{\mathcal{S}}
and the identity object
1\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

III
SymTFT Sandwich

A sandwich
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
is obtained by supplying a quiche
\mathcal{Q}_{d}=(\mathfrak{B}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1})
with a (possibly non-topological) boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{d}
, known as the physical boundary, on the right

\mathfrak{S}_{d}=(\mathfrak{B}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1},\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{d})\,.

(III.1)

In the context of SymTFT, we express the sandwich as

\mathfrak{S}_{d}=\left(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}),\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}\right)

(III.2)

which can be depicted as

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}
=
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
{\mathcal{S}}

(III.3)

whose interval compactification produces a
d
-dimensional QFT
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
with symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
. Conversely, any
d
-dimensional QFT
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
with symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
can be expressed as such a sandwich. We have a one-to-one correspondence

{
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric QFTs}
{Physical boundaries of the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
}

(III.4)

III.1
Generalized Charges

As discussed in detail in
Bhardwaj and Schafer-Nameki 2023
, the topological
(q+1)
-dimensional operators of the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
capture the charges of
q
-dimensional operators appearing in an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional QFT.

Let us briefly review how this comes about. Any (possibly non-topological)
q
-dimensional operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
in a
d
-dimensional
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric QFT
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
is charged under the symmetry only if it somehow interacts with the symmetry boundary. This is possible only if the sandwich construction of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
involves a bulk topological
(q+1)
-dimensional operator
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
ending on the physical boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}
along a (possibly non-topological)
q
-dimensional operator
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
as shown below

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}
=
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
{\mathcal{S}}
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}

(III.5)

The end
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
of
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
is a topological
q
-dimensional operator along the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
, which may be attached to topological operators living on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
(in which case
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is also attached to topological operators in
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
generating the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
and hence lives in twisted sector for the symmetry). The action of the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is captured in how the bulk operator
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
interacts with the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
(via the end
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
), and hence
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
captures the charge of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
under
{\mathcal{S}}
. Note that if there is no end
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
of a bulk topological operator
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
on a physical boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{d}}
, then there is no
q
-dimensional operator in the theory
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
carrying the generalized charge
\mathbf{Q}_{q+1}
.

III.2
Gapped Phases with Non-Invertible Symmetries

Consider a categorical symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
. One can define an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional gapped phase as an equivalence class of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional gapped systems, in which two such systems are regarded to be equivalent if one of the systems can be deformed into the other without closing the gap (even at infinite volume) and without losing
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetry at any point along the deformation path.
Note that an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase
\left[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\right]
has an underlying (non-symmetric) gapped phase
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}]

\left[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\right]\mapsto[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}]\,,

(III.6)

where
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}]
is the gapped phase occupied by any
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric system
\mathfrak{Z}_{d}
lying in the
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase
\left[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\right]
.
Multiple
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases
\left[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\right]_{i}
may have the same underlying gapped phase
[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}]

\left[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\right]_{i}\mapsto[\mathfrak{Z}_{d}],\qquad\forall~i\,.

(III.7)

One can identify a bosonic
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped
d
-dimensional phase as a deformation class of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional TQFTs. An irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase is one that cannot be expressed as a sum of other
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases. Equivalently, the only local operators left invariant by
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry in an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric phase are multiples of the identity operator. It should be noted that the non-symmetric gapped phase underlying an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase may be reducible.

In terms of the SymTFT sandwich, an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}
is obtained by supplying an irreducible topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{Z}_{d}^{{\mathcal{S}}}}
of
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
as the physical boundary. Thus, an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase is a deformation class of SymTFT sandwiches

\left[\mathfrak{S}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{d}\right]=\left(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}),\left[\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}\right]\right)\,,

(III.8)

where
\left[\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}\right]
is a deformation class of irreducible topological boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
of
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
that are used as physical boundary conditions.

For
d=2
, as discussed previously, such a deformation class is described by a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
in the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. We thus have the one-to-one correspondence

{(1+1)d irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases}
{Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
}

(III.9)

As discussed in the previous subsection, the possible generalized charges appearing in an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric theory
\mathfrak{T}
correspond to the bulk topological operators that can end along the physical boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. For an irreducible (1+1)d
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase
\left[\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathcal{S}}\right](\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}})
, these are captured by the corresponding Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
: the simple anyons
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
appearing in
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
are the generalized charges carried by local operators appearing in
\left[\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathcal{S}}\right](\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}})
. In other words, these are the generalized charges carried by
order parameters
for the
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase
\left[\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathcal{S}}\right](\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}})
, i.e. the operators having non-trivial vacuum expectation value (vev) in the phase
\left[\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathcal{S}}\right](\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}})
.

Mathematically, the classification of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped phases is the classification of deformation classes of certain pivotal 2-functors. See appendix
A
for more details.

IV
Club Quiche

A
d
-dimensional club quiche
\mathcal{Q}^{C}_{d}
is a tuple

\mathcal{Q}^{C}_{d}=(\mathfrak{B}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1},{\mathcal{I}}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1})\,,

(IV.1)

where
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
and
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
are
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFTs,
\mathfrak{B}_{d}
is a topological boundary condition of
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
is a topological interface from
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
to
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
. We will often use it in the context of SymTFT, where the club quiche takes the form

\mathcal{Q}^{C}_{d}=\left(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}}),{\mathcal{I}}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}\right)

(IV.2)

and we depict it as

\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
{\mathcal{I}}_{d}
=
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{d}
{\mathcal{S}}

(IV.3)

where compactifying the interval occupied by the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
leads to an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric quiche

\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}_{d}=(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{d},\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1})

(IV.4)

with the
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetry being realized on a topological boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{d}
of the TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
. Conversely, any topological boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{d}
with symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
can be expressed as such a club quiche. We have a one-to-one correspondence

{
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric Topological Boundaries of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
}
{Topological Interfaces from SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})
to
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
}

(IV.5)

Such topological interfaces are the same as topological boundary conditions of the folded
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}_{d+1}
, where
\overline{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}_{d+1}
is the
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFT obtained by reflecting
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
, and the boxtimes operation
\boxtimes
denotes the stacking of TQFTs.

IV.1
Gapped Boundary Phases with Non-Invertible Symmetries

Physically, such a club quiche can be understood as characterizing gapped boundary phases with
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetry, where the symmetry is localized completely along the boundary. We do not incorporate any symmetry in the bulk, but will consider situations where both bulk and boundaries are symmetric in an upcoming work
Bhardwaj et al. 2024c
.

An
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional gapped boundary phase is defined as an equivalence class of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped bulk+boundary systems (where the bulk is
(d+1)
-dimensional), in which two such systems are regarded to be equivalent if one of them can be deformed into the other without closing the gap in the bulk or on the boundary (even when both bulk and boundary have infinite volume) and without losing
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetry at any point along the deformation path. One can identify an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phase as a deformation class of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional quiches, and so we denote an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped
d
-dimensional boundary phase as
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}^{\mathcal{S}}]
.

Note that
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}^{\mathcal{S}}]
has an underlying (non-symmetric) gapped boundary phase
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]

[\mathcal{Q}_{d}^{\mathcal{S}}]\mapsto[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]\,,

(IV.6)

where
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}]
is the gapped boundary phase occupied by any
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric bulk+boundary system lying in the
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase
[\mathcal{Q}_{d}^{\mathcal{S}}]
.

An irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase is one that cannot be expressed as a sum of other
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phases. Equivalently, the only local operators living on the boundary left invariant by
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry in an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric boundary phase are multiples of the identity operator. It should be noted that the non-symmetric gapped boundary phase underlying an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phase may be reducible.

For
d=2
, one can characterize an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase as

[\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}]=(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}}),[{\mathcal{I}}],\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime})\,,

(IV.7)

where
[{\mathcal{I}}]
is a deformation class of topological interfaces from the 3d SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
to a 3d TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
. An irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase is then characterized by a deformation class of irreducible topological interfaces from
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
to an irreducible 3d TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
, which by folding is the same as a deformation class of irreducible topological boundary conditions of the irreducible 3d TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
. As we discussed earlier, such a deformation class of boundary conditions is characterized by a Lagrangian algebra in the MTC
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is the Drinfeld center of
{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
is the MTC formed by topological lines of
\overline{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
. We are thus led to one-to-one correspondence

{
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phases w/ bulk phase
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
}
{
Lagrangian algebras in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
}

(IV.8)

Consider a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
characterizing an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase
\left[\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\right]
. It can be expressed as

\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}=\bigoplus_{a,a^{\prime}}n_{a,a^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{a},\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}\right)\,,

(IV.9)

where
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
are simple anyons in
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
and
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
are simple anyons in
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
. Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be an irreducible topological interface in the deformation class
[{\mathcal{I}}]
. The presence of a term
n_{a,a^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{Q}_{a},\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}\right)
in
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
means that there is an
n_{a,a^{\prime}}
-dimensional vector space of topological local operators along
{\mathcal{I}}
acting as line changing operators from the line
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
to the line
(\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}})^{*}
, which is the dual of
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
in the MTC
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
, or in other words the orientation reversed version of it

\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
{\mathcal{I}}
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
(\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}})^{*}
n_{a,a^{\prime}}
dimensional

(IV.10)

After contracting
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
, such an operator descends to a topological local operator along the resulting
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
, which is attached to the bulk anyon
(\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}})^{*}
, and carries a generalized charge
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
under the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
acting on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
. This may be regarded as an order parameter in the
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
-anyon sector for the resulting
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped boundary phase
\left[\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\right]
. Thus, the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
captures the order parameters for the associated
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase.

Using the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
, one can also deduce the underlying non-symmetric gapped boundary phase

[\mathcal{Q}_{{\mathcal{I}}}]=\left([\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}],\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}\right)\,.

(IV.11)

A topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
in the deformation class
[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]
is in general reducible

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{i}\,,

(IV.12)

where
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{i}
are irreducible topological boundary conditions of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
. We can characterize
[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]
in terms of a “Lagrangian algebra”
\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]}

\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]}:=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{i}]}\,,

(IV.13)

where
\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{i}]}
are Lagrangian algebras associated to deformation classes
[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{i}]
of irreducible topological boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{i}
. This is related to
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
via

\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]}=\bigoplus_{a,a^{\prime}}n^{\text{sym}}_{a^{*}}n_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}\,,

(IV.14)

where
n_{a,a^{\prime}}
are the coefficients appearing in the Lagrangian algebra (
IV.9
) associated to the interface and
n^{\text{sym}}_{a^{*}}
is the coefficient for
\mathbf{Q}_{a}^{*}
appearing in the Lagrangian algebra (
II.18
) associated to the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

We will further restrict the studies in this paper to
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
minimal
(1+1)d gapped boundary phases, which are special types of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric irreducible (1+1)d gapped boundary phases. In order to define such phases, note that the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
specifies a non-Lagrangian condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
and a non-Lagrangian condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
, which are obtained respectively by restricting
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
to
\overline{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}_{a^{\prime}}=1
or to
\mathbf{Q}_{a}=1
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{a}n_{a,1}\mathbf{Q}_{a}

(IV.15)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{a^{\prime}}n_{1,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}\,.

These condensable algebras capture the possible ends along a topological interface
{\mathcal{I}}
in class
[{\mathcal{I}}]
of topological bulk lines from left and right

\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
{\mathcal{I}}
\mathbf{Q}_{a}
n_{a,1}
dimensional
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
{\mathcal{I}}
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
n_{1,a^{\prime}}
dimensional

(IV.16)

Note that, if the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})
is non-trivial, then we have non-identity topological local operators in the irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase
\left[\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\right]
that are completely uncharged under
{\mathcal{S}}
. This is because the
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry is captured by topological lines living on the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
while a topological operator of the type appearing on the right hand side of (
IV.16
) does not interact with the symmetry boundary. This means that there is physical information in such a phase that is disconnected from the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
, e.g. some of the order parameters for such a phase carry trivial generalized charges under
{\mathcal{S}}
. We are thus led to define a
minimal
irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase
\left[\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\right]
to be a phase specified by a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
whose associated condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is trivial, i.e.
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})=1
. Correspondingly we call such a Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
as a
minimal Lagrangian
.

One can also define minimal club quiches and minimal
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases even in higher spacetime dimensions by requiring that no topological line operators of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}_{d+1}
can end on the topological interface
{\mathcal{I}}
.

The minimal
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped boundary phases are classified (upto equivalences) by condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. Pick a condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
, then the associated gapped bulk phase
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
is determined by computing local
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})

Kong 2014
, which form a “smaller” modular tensor category
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})/{\mathcal{A}}

\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})/{\mathcal{A}}\,.

(IV.17)

The deformation class
[{\mathcal{I}}]
of topological interfaces is then determined by picking a Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})/{\mathcal{A}}}

(IV.18)

such that the condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
associated to
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
is the same as
{\mathcal{A}}

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{I}})={\mathcal{A}}\,.

(IV.19)

There may be multiple possibilities for
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
satisfying the above condition, but they are all related by the action of some 0-form symmetry of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
, which are auto-equivalences that do not change the physical properties of the resulting minimal
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped boundary phase.

There are a couple of special choices for the condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
:

•

First of all, we can choose the trivial algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=1\,,

(IV.20)

which corresponds to the deformation class containing the identity interface
{\mathcal{I}}
. The corresponding minimal
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase is simply the phase in which the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
lies. The associated gapped bulk phase is

\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\,.

(IV.21)

•

We can also choose a Lagrangian algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\,.

(IV.22)

In particular, for any
{\mathcal{S}}
we have at least the choice
{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
where
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the Lagrangian algebra for the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
. In this case the minimal
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped boundary phase is simply an irreducible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped phase, i.e. the associated (2+1)d gapped bulk phase is trivial

\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}=1\,.

(IV.23)

The
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped phase is the one obtained by performing a sandwich compactification of the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
with the physical boundary specified by the Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}=\mathcal{L}\,.

(IV.24)

More details about such phases can be found in
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
.

IV.2
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
Symmetry

Let us find (1+1)d
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-symmetric minimal gapped boundary phases.
In this case the symmetry is

{\mathcal{S}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}

(IV.25)

with simple objects

\{1,P,P^{2},P^{3}\}\,,

(IV.26)

and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is the 3d
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
Dijkgraaf-Witten gauge theory without twist (sometimes also called the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
toric code). Let us label the anyons in this theory by

e^{i}m^{j},\qquad i,j\in\{0,1,2,3\}\,.

(IV.27)

The bosons are the electric lines
e^{i}
, the magnetic lines
m^{j}
, and the dyonic line
e^{2}m^{2}
. We are interested in non-trivial condensable algebras that are non-Lagrangian. There are three possibilities

{\mathcal{A}}_{e}=1\oplus e^{2},\quad{\mathcal{A}}_{m}=1\oplus m^{2},\quad{\mathcal{A}}_{em}=1\oplus e^{2}m^{2}\,.

(IV.28)

We discuss these three possibilities in turn. The symmetry boundary is taken to be specified by the Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{3}e^{i}\,.

(IV.29)

IV.2.1
Condensable Algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{e}

In this case, one can compute that the bulk phase
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
is the toric code

\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})/{\mathcal{A}}_{e}=\{1,e^{\prime},m^{\prime},e^{\prime}m^{\prime}\}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})

(IV.30)

This can be equivalently seen by noting that the Lagrangian algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}=

\displaystyle 1\oplus e\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{3}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\oplus m^{2}\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\oplus em^{2}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\overline{m}^{\prime}

(IV.31)

\displaystyle\oplus e^{2}m^{2}\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\oplus e^{3}m^{2}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\overline{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}\,,

is minimal, and its associated condensable algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})
matches
{\mathcal{A}}_{e}

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})}(\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}})={\mathcal{A}}_{e}\,.

(IV.32)

We can also compute the algebra in
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
corresponding to the underlying non-symmetric gapped boundary phase to be

\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]}=2(1\oplus e^{\prime})\,,

(IV.33)

which turns out to be the direct sum of two electric Lagrangian algebras, and therefore decomposable. This in turn means that the topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
produced by colliding
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
with
{\mathcal{I}}
is reducible, and can be expressed as

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}\,,

(IV.34)

where
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
is an irreducible topological boundary condition lying in the deformation class
[\mathfrak{B}](\mathcal{L}_{e})
associated to Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}_{e}=1\oplus e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\,.

(IV.35)

The simple topological line operators on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
are

1_{ij},\quad P_{ij},\qquad i,j\in\{0,1\}\,,

(IV.36)

where
1_{ii}
is the identity line on
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
;
P_{ii}
is the line generating
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
;
1_{01},1_{10}
are boundary changing line operators satisfying

1_{ij}\otimes 1_{jk}=1_{ik}

(IV.37)

and
P_{01},P_{10}
are another set of boundary changing line operators obtained by fusing with
P_{ii}
lines

P_{ii}\otimes 1_{ij}=1_{ij}\otimes P_{jj}=P_{ij}\,.

(IV.38)

We can recognize various boundary operators in terms of the operators descending from the club quiche:

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}}
{\mathcal{A}}_{e}
e
e^{\prime}
e^{2}
e^{3}
e^{\prime}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
2\mathcal{L}_{e}
e^{\prime}
e^{\prime}
{\mathcal{E}}_{+}
{\mathcal{O}}_{-}
{\mathcal{E}}_{-}
=

(IV.39)

The products of these operators are

{\mathcal{E}}_{s}^{2}={\mathcal{O}}_{-},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{s}{\mathcal{O}}_{-}={\mathcal{E}}_{-s},\qquad{\mathcal{O}}_{-}^{2}=1

(IV.40)

for
s\in\{+,-\}
, which we can relate as

\displaystyle v_{0}=\frac{1+{\mathcal{O}}_{-}}{2},

\displaystyle v_{1}=\frac{1-{\mathcal{O}}_{-}}{2}

(IV.41)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{0}=\frac{{\mathcal{E}}_{+}+{\mathcal{E}}_{-}}{2},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{E}}_{1}=i\,\frac{{\mathcal{E}}_{+}-{\mathcal{E}}_{-}}{2}\,,

where
v_{i}
are the identity local operators along the irreducible boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
are the ends of
e^{\prime}
along boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
. Note that the overlap between the operators on two different boundaries is indeed trivial, as required by consistency

v_{0}v_{1}=0,\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{0}v_{1}=0,\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{1}v_{0}=0,\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{0}{\mathcal{E}}_{1}=0

(IV.42)

and the
v_{i}
are indeed identity along the boundaries as we have

v_{i}v_{i}=v_{i},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{i}v_{i}={\mathcal{E}}_{i},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{i}{\mathcal{E}}_{i}=v_{i}\,.

(IV.43)

The linking action of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry is known on the ends of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})
anyons, which descends to the following linking action

P:~{\mathcal{E}}_{s}\to is{\mathcal{E}}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{-}\to-{\mathcal{O}}_{-},\quad 1\to 1\,,

(IV.44)

implying that we have

P:~v_{0}\leftrightarrow v_{1},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{1},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\to-{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\,.

(IV.45)

A topological line operator having such an action exists on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
only if its components
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
are the same boundaries, i.e. there is no relative Euler term between
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}

\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}=\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}=\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{e})

(IV.46)

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. This parameter
\lambda
drops out at the level of boundary phases where only deformation classes of boundary conditions matter. Given (
IV.46
), the linking action of lines on
v_{i}
of lines living on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is

X_{ij}:~v_{i}\to v_{j},\qquad X\in\{1,P\}

(IV.47)

and the linking action on
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
is

\displaystyle 1_{ij}

\displaystyle:~{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{j}

(IV.48)

\displaystyle P_{ij}

\displaystyle:~{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\to-{\mathcal{E}}_{j}\,.

We can then identify the line operator on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
generating the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry as

\phi(P)=1_{01}\oplus P_{10}\,.

(IV.49)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
is generated by

\phi(P^{2})=[\phi(P)]^{2}=P_{00}\oplus P_{11}

(IV.50)

and the consistency condition

\phi(P^{4})=\phi(1)=1

(IV.51)

is satisfied as

\phi(P^{4})=[\phi(P)]^{4}=1_{00}\oplus 1_{11}=1\,.

(IV.52)

Note that the identity line on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is the sum
1_{00}\oplus 1_{11}
on identity lines on
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}
.

Mathematically, we have provided information of a pivotal tensor functor

\phi:~\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\to\text{Mat}_{2}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})

(IV.53)

where
\text{Mat}_{2}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
is the multi-fusion category formed by
2\times 2
matrices in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, and is physically the category formed by topological line operators living on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
. This functor describes a choice of lines on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
generating
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry, and is the mathematical characterization of the minimal
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped boundary phase under study. See appendix
A
for more details.

Note that, at the beginning of this analysis, we could have picked another Lagrangian algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{[{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}]}=

\displaystyle 1\oplus e\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{3}\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\oplus m^{2}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\oplus em^{2}\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\overline{e}^{\prime}

(IV.54)

\displaystyle\oplus e^{2}m^{2}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\oplus e^{3}m^{2}\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\overline{e}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}\,,

whose associated condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})}(\mathcal{L}_{[{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}]})
is also
{\mathcal{A}}_{e}
. This choice leads to the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-symmetry being realized on

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{m}\oplus\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{m}

(IV.55)

where

\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{m}=\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{m}=\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{m})

(IV.56)

and
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{m})
is an irreducible topological boundary of the toric code associated to the Lagrangian algebra
1\oplus m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
. The lines living on such a
\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{m}\oplus\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{m}
have identical properties as the lines living on
\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{e}\oplus\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{e}
, and hence we obtain an equivalent minimal
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-symmetric gapped boundary phase. This is related to the fact that the irreducible boundaries
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{e})
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{m})
are exchanged by the action of a 0-form symmetry of the toric code, namely the electric-magnetic symmetry exchanging the electric and magnetic lines
e^{\prime}\leftrightarrow m^{\prime}
.

IV.2.2
Condensable Algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}

In this case,
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
is again the
toric code and we can take

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}=

\displaystyle 1\oplus m\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\oplus m^{2}\oplus m^{3}\,\overline{m}^{\prime}\oplus e^{2}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\oplus e^{2}m\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\overline{m}^{\prime}

(IV.57)

\displaystyle\oplus e^{2}m^{2}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\oplus e^{2}m^{3}\,\overline{e}^{\prime}\overline{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}\,.

The Lagrangian algebra associated to the underlying non-symmetric gapped boundary phase is computed to be

\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]}=1\oplus e^{\prime}

(IV.58)

which means that
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is an irreducible topological boundary on which the electric anyon
e^{\prime}
is condensed, i.e.

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{e})

(IV.59)

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Again, the parameter
\lambda
will not appear at the level of phases. As discussed earlier, the topological lines on the boundary are

1,\qquad P^{\prime}

(IV.60)

where 1 is the identity line and
P^{\prime}
generates
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry localized on the boundary.

The operators descending from the club quiche are

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}}

(IV.61)

with the action of

P:~{\mathcal{E}}\to-{\mathcal{E}}\,.

(IV.62)

We can thus recognize the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry generator on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
as

\phi(P)=P^{\prime}

(IV.63)

which indeed satisfies the consistency condition

\phi(P^{4})=1

(IV.64)

because
[\phi(P)]^{4}=1
.

Mathematically, we have provided a pivotal tensor functor

\phi:~\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

(IV.65)

where
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is the fusion category formed by topological lines living on the boundary

. All the data of this functor simply descends from the non-trivial homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{4}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

IV.2.3
Condensable Algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{em}

For the condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{em}=1\oplus e^{2}m^{2}
,
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
is the double semion, which is also the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega})
for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry with a non-trivial ’t Hooft anomaly

\omega\neq 0\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},U(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\,.

(IV.66)

That is, the anyons of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
are

\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})/{\mathcal{A}}_{em}=\left\{1,s,\overline{s},s\overline{s}\right\}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}^{\omega}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})\,,

(IV.67)

where
s
is a semion,
\overline{s}
is an anti-semion and
s\overline{s}
is a boson.

We can take the Lagrangian algebra completion of
{\mathcal{A}}_{em}
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}=

\displaystyle 1\oplus em\,\overline{s}\oplus e^{2}m^{2}\oplus e^{3}m^{3}\,\overline{s}\oplus em^{3}\,s\oplus e^{2}\,s\overline{s}

(IV.68)

\displaystyle\oplus e^{3}m\,s\oplus m^{2}\,s\overline{s}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}

from which we find the Lagrangian algebra for the underlying non-symmetric gapped boundary phase to be

\mathcal{L}_{[\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}]}=\mathcal{L}_{s\overline{s}}=1\oplus s\overline{s}\,,

(IV.69)

which corresponds to an irreducible topological boundary condition of the double semion on which the anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry is realized, that is

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{s\overline{s}})

(IV.70)

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. The line operators living on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
are

1,\qquad P^{\prime}\,,

(IV.71)

\phi:~\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\to\mathsf{Vec}^{\omega}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\,.

(IV.82)

IV.3
S_{3}
Symmetry

Let us now discuss the non-trivial, minimal (1+1)d
S_{3}
-symmetric gapped boundary phases. Here the symmetry is

{\mathcal{S}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}=\{1,a,a^{2},b,ab,a^{2}b\}\,,

(IV.83)

where

b^{2}=1\,,\qquad a^{3}=1\,,\qquad ab=ba^{2}\,.

(IV.84)

The SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
for this symmetry is then the 3d
S_{3}
Dijkgraaf-Witten gauge theory without a twist (which can be obtained from the 3d
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
DW gauge theory by gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
automorphism symmetry, which exchanges the pairs
(e,m)\leftrightarrow(e^{2},m^{2})
). The anyons of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})
are labeled by conjugacy classes of
S_{3}
and the irreducible representations of the centralizers of these conjugacy classes:

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})=\{1,\,1_{-},\,E,\,a_{1},\,a_{\omega},\,a_{\omega^{2}},\,b_{+},\,b_{-}\}\,,

(IV.85)

where
1_{-}
is the sign representation of
S_{3}
,
E
the 2d irreducible representation of
S_{3}
,
\omega=e^{\pm 2\pi i/3}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
irreducible representation, and
+
,
-
denote the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
irreducible representations. The bosonic lines in this case are

1,\,1_{-},\,E,\,a_{1},\,b_{+}\,,

(IV.86)

from which one can construct the condensable algebras of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})
. There are three condensable, non-Lagrangian, algebras

{\mathcal{A}}_{1_{-}}=1\oplus 1_{-},\quad{\mathcal{A}}_{E}=1\oplus E,\quad{\mathcal{A}}_{a_{1}}=1\oplus a_{1}\,,

(IV.87)

which we now study case by case.
Note that the algebra
1\oplus b_{+}
is not a condensable. This can been seen e.g. from the fact that it does not satisfy the condition (CA3) in appendix
B
.

For the symmetry boundary we fix a Lagrangian algebra which corresponds to the
S_{3}
symmetry,

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}}=1\oplus 1_{-}\oplus 2E\,.

(IV.88)

IV.3.1
Condensable Algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{1_{-}}

In this case, we have that
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
is the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
DW theory with anyon content

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}/{\mathcal{A}}_{1_{-}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})

(IV.89)

\displaystyle=\{1,e,e^{2},m,m^{2},em,e^{2}m,em^{2},e^{2}m^{2}\}\,.

A Lagrangian algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(S_{3})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
which completes
{\mathcal{A}}_{1_{-}}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}=

\displaystyle 1\oplus 1_{-}\oplus a_{1}\overline{m}\oplus a_{1}\overline{m}^{2}\oplus a_{\omega^{2}}\overline{e}\overline{m}\oplus

(IV.90)

\displaystyle\oplus a_{\omega^{2}}\overline{e}^{2}\overline{m}^{2}\oplus E\overline{e}\oplus E\overline{e}^{2}\oplus a_{\omega}\overline{e}\overline{m}^{2}\oplus a_{\omega}\overline{e}^{2}\overline{m}\,.

The decomposable algebra for the underlying non-symmetric gapped boundary phase is obtained by following the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}}
lines and seeing what they transform into via
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
; this gives

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}}=2(1\oplus e\oplus e^{2})\,.

(IV.91)

This implies that the underlying boundary of the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
DW theory is reducible and given by

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}\,,

(IV.92)

where
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
is an irreducible topological boundary condition lying in the deformation class
[\mathfrak{B}](\mathcal{L}_{e})
associated to Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}_{e}=1\oplus e\oplus e^{2}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\,.

(IV.93)

The simple topological lines on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
are

1_{ij},\quad P_{ij},\quad P^{2}_{ij},\qquad i,j\in\{0,1\}\,.

(IV.94)

where
1_{ii}
is the identity line on
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
;
P_{ii}
is a line generating
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry of
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
;
P^{2}_{ii}
is the square of
P_{ii}
;
1_{01},1_{10}
are boundary changing line operators satisfying

1_{ij}\otimes 1_{jk}=1_{ik}\,.

(IV.95)

P_{01},P_{10}
are another set of boundary changing line operators obtained by fusing with
P_{ii}
lines

P_{ii}\otimes 1_{ij}=1_{ij}\otimes P_{jj}=P_{ij}

(IV.96)

and
P^{2}_{01},P^{2}_{10}
are boundary changing line operators obtained by fusing with
P^{2}_{ii}
lines

P^{2}_{ii}\otimes 1_{ij}=1_{ij}\otimes P^{2}_{jj}=P^{2}_{ij}\,.

(IV.97)

We can recognize various boundary operators in terms of operators descending from the club quiche

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}}
{\mathcal{A}}_{1_{-}}
E
e^{2}
E
e
1_{-}
=
e^{2}
e
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})
2\mathcal{L}_{e}
{\mathcal{E}}^{1,2}_{e^{2}}
{\mathcal{E}}^{1,2}_{e}
{\mathcal{O}}_{-}

(IV.98)

where we obtain two operators
{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{2}}^{i}
and two operators
{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{2}}^{i}
because there are two possible ends of the
E
line along the symmetry boundary denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}}
. The products of these operators are determined to be

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{-}^{2}=1,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{O}}_{-}{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}={\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1},\qquad{\mathcal{O}}_{-}{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}=-{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}

(IV.99)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}={\mathcal{E}}_{e^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}={\mathcal{E}}_{e^{2}}^{1},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}=0\,,

which implies we can just focus on
{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}
as
{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{2}}^{i}
is related to the square of
{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{j}
. These products are determined in a similar way as in
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
, by demanding that

•

their product is consistent with the fusions of attached bulk anyons,

•

their product is associative,

•

their product is consistent with the action of topological lines living on the boundaries, and

•

performing rescalings of these operators to put the product in a neat form.

In the relatively simple examples considered in this work, the above necessary conditions allow us to solve completely the algebra of boundary operators. However, we remark that in more complicated examples there could be potentially multiple solutions. In these instances a complete knowledge of the condensable algebra, including the multiplication structure, is required.

The identity local operators along the irreducible boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}
are

v_{0}=\frac{1+{\mathcal{O}}_{-}}{2}\quad,\quad v_{1}=\frac{1-{\mathcal{O}}_{-}}{2}\,.

(IV.100)

{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}
is then the end of
e
along
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}
is the end of
e
along
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}
. This is due to the products

\displaystyle v_{i}v_{j}=\delta_{ij}v_{j},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}v_{0}={\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}v_{1}=0

(IV.101)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}v_{0}=0,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}v_{1}={\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{1}{\mathcal{E}}_{e}^{2}=0\,.

The linking action of the
S_{3}
symmetry generators are

b:\quad\begin{aligned} {\mathcal{E}}_{e^{i}}^{1}&\leftrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{i}}^{2}\cr{\mathcal{O}}_{-}&\rightarrow-{\mathcal{O}}_{-}\end{aligned}\qquad a:\quad\begin{aligned} {\mathcal{E}}_{e^{i}}^{1}&\rightarrow\omega{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{i}}^{1}\cr{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{i}}^{2}&\rightarrow\omega^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{e^{i}}^{2}\cr{\mathcal{O}}_{-}&\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{-}\,.\end{aligned}

(IV.102)

which identifies the
S_{3}
generators to be the following line operators on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\phi(a)

\displaystyle=P_{00}\oplus P^{2}_{11}

(IV.103)

\displaystyle\phi(b)

\displaystyle=1_{01}\oplus 1_{10}

and constrains the relative Euler term between the boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}
to be trivial

\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}=\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}=\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}_{e})

(IV.104)

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
The reader can check that the lines
\phi(a),\phi(b)
satisfy all the
S_{3}
relations.

Mathematically, we have provided information of a pivotal tensor functor

\phi:~\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}\to\text{Mat}_{2}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}})

(IV.105)

where physically
\text{Mat}_{2}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}})
is the multi-fusion the category formed by topological line operators living on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
. This functor describes a choice of lines on
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
generating
S_{3}
symmetry, and is the mathematical characterization of the minimal
S_{3}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped boundary phase under study. See appendix
A
for more details.

IV.3.2
Condensable Algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{E}

In this case, we have that
\mathfrak{Z}^{\prime}
is the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
DW theory (i.e. toric code)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}/{\mathcal{A}}_{E}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})=\{1,e,m,f\}\,.

(IV.106)

A Lagrangian algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(S_{3})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
which completes
{\mathcal{A}}_{E}
is

\mathcal{L}_{I}=1\oplus E\oplus 1_{-}\overline{e}\oplus E\overline{e}\oplus b_{+}\overline{m}\oplus b_{-}\overline{f}\,.

(IV.107)

The decomposable algebra for the underlying non-symmetric gapped boundary phase is obtained by following the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}}
lines and seeing what they transform into via
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{I}}}
; this gives

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}}=3(1\oplus e)\,.

(IV.108)

This implies that the underlying boundary of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
DW theory is reducible and given by

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{2}\,,

(IV.109)

where
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
is an irreducible topological boundary condition lying in the deformation class
[\mathfrak{B}](\mathcal{L}_{e})
associated to Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{e}=1\oplus e
.

The topological lines on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
are

1_{ij},\quad P_{ij},\qquad i,j\in\{0,1\}\,.

(IV.110)

where
1_{ii}
is the identity line on
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
;
P_{ii}
is the line generating
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of
\mathfrak{B}^{e}_{i}
;
1_{ij}
for
i\neq j
are boundary changing line operators satisfying

1_{ij}\otimes 1_{jk}=1_{ik}\,

(IV.111)

and
P_{ij}
for
i\neq j
are another set of boundary changing line operators obtained by fusing with
P_{ii}
lines

P_{ii}\otimes 1_{ij}=1_{ij}\otimes P_{jj}=P_{ij}\,.

(IV.112)

The various boundary operators in terms of operators descending from the club quiche are

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}}
{\mathcal{A}}_{E}

(IV.113)

The products of these operators are determined to be

\begin{aligned} {\mathcal{E}}_{-}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}&=1,\\
\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{1}&={\mathcal{O}}_{2},\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}&={\mathcal{O}}_{1},\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}&=1,\\

{\mathcal{E}}_{-}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}&=-{\mathcal{E}}_{2},\\
{\mathcal{E}}_{-}{\mathcal{E}}_{1}&={\mathcal{O}}_{1},\\
{\mathcal{E}}_{-}{\mathcal{E}}_{2}&=-{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\\
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}&={\mathcal{E}}_{-},\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} {\mathcal{E}}_{1}{\mathcal{E}}_{1}&={\mathcal{O}}_{2},\\

From these we construct the operators

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\omega^{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{1}+\omega^{2i}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}}{3}

(IV.115)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{E}}_{-}+\omega^{i}{\mathcal{E}}_{1}-\omega^{2i}{\mathcal{E}}_{2}}{3}={\mathcal{E}}_{-}v_{i}\,,

(IV.116)

derived using the above product rules.

The linking actions of the
S_{3}

b:\quad\begin{aligned} {\mathcal{O}}_{1}&\leftrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\cr{\mathcal{E}}_{1}&\leftrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\cr{\mathcal{E}}_{-}&\rightarrow-{\mathcal{E}}_{-}\end{aligned}\qquad a:\quad\begin{aligned} {\mathcal{O}}_{k}&\rightarrow\omega^{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}\cr{\mathcal{E}}_{k}&\rightarrow\omega^{k}{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\cr{\mathcal{E}}_{-}&\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}_{-}\,,\end{aligned}

(IV.117)

for

, from which we find the linking actions

b:\quad\begin{aligned} v_{0}&\rightarrow v_{0}\cr v_{1}&\rightarrow v_{2}\cr v_{2}&\rightarrow v_{1}\cr\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}&\rightarrow-\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\cr\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}&\rightarrow-\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\cr\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}&\rightarrow-\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\end{aligned}\qquad a:\quad\begin{aligned} v_{0}&\rightarrow v_{1}\cr v_{1}&\rightarrow v_{2}\cr v_{2}&\rightarrow v_{0}\cr\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}&\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cr\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}&\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\cr\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}&\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\,.\end{aligned}

which identifies the
S_{3}

\displaystyle\phi(a)

\displaystyle=1_{01}\oplus 1_{12}\oplus 1_{20}

(IV.119)

\displaystyle\phi(b)

\displaystyle=P_{00}\oplus P_{12}\oplus P_{21}

S_{3}

Mathematically, we have provided information of a pivotal tensor functor

(IV.121)
