trivial phase, and hence we land on the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SSB phase discussed above. On the other hand, adding this operator with negative sign sends
\text{Ising}_{e}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
trivial phase and
\text{Ising}_{m}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase, and hence we land on the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase discussed above.

The order parameters for the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
SSB phase
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]
involve a
1_{-}
-multiplet and two distinct
a_{1}
-multiplets of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. Applying KT transformations (
V.108
) and (
V.109
) respectively to the identity operator
id
and the spin operator
\sigma
of the Ising CFT, we deduce that these order parameters are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},u}

\displaystyle=\text{id}_{e}-2\text{id}_{m}

(VI.154)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\text{id}_{e}}{1+2\omega}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{1_{-}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{e}+\mu_{m}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}_{a_{1},u}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\sigma_{e}}{1+2\omega}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}_{a_{1},t}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{e}-2\mu_{m}\,,

where
\{{\mathcal{O}}^{(k)}_{a_{1},u},{\mathcal{O}}^{(k)}_{a_{1},t}\}
for
k=0,1
are the two
a_{1}
-multiplets and the operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{1_{-}}
comprises the
1_{-}
-multiplet. The IR images of above operators are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},u}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}+v_{1}-2v_{2}

(VI.155)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},t}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{3(v_{0}+v_{1})}{1+2\omega}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{1_{-}}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}+v_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}_{a_{1},u}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{3(v_{0}-v_{1})}{1+2\omega}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}_{a_{1},t}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}-2v_{2}\,.

The order parameters for the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2}]
involve an
a_{1}
-multiplet and a
b_{+}
-multiplet of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. Applying KT transformations (
V.108
) and (
V.110
) respectively to the identity operator
id
and the disorder operator
\mu
of the Ising CFT, we deduce that these order parameters are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},u}

\displaystyle=\text{id}_{e}-2\text{id}_{m}

(VI.156)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\text{id}_{e}}{1+2\omega}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{m}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+},+}

\displaystyle=\mu_{e}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+},-}

\displaystyle=\mu_{e}\,,

where
\{{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},u},{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},t}\}
is the
a_{1}
-multiplet and
\{{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+}},{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+},+},{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+},-}\}
the
b_{+}
-multiplet. The IR images of above operators are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},u}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-2v_{1}-2v_{2}

(VI.157)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}_{a_{1},t}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{3v_{0}}{1+2\omega}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+}}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{1}-v_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+},+}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{b_{+},-}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}\,.

VI.11
Phase Transition between two
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB Phases for
\mathsf{Ising}
Symmetry

Now consider the KT transformation studied in section
V.11
. This KT transformation converts
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry to
\mathsf{Ising}=\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
symmetry. We use the Ising CFT to now obtain an
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric phase transition described below.

Using the map (
V.119
), we can quickly deduce that the
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric gapped phases obtained after applying KT transformation on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SSB and trivial phases for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry are described respectively by the physical Lagrangian algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{1}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus P\overline{P}\oplus S\overline{S}

(VI.158)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus P\overline{P}\oplus S\overline{S}\,.

Thus, we have the same
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric gapped phase on both sides of the transition, which is the only possible irreducible
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric gapped phase that we refer to as the
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase

\displaystyle=\text{$\mathsf{Ising}$ SSB phase for $\mathsf{Ising}$ symmetry}

(VI.159)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{2}]

\displaystyle=\text{$\mathsf{Ising}$ SSB phase for $\mathsf{Ising}$ symmetry}

For more details on this gapped phase, we refer the reader to
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
.

Another equivalent way to deduce this
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric gapped phase is to apply the results of section
V.11
. Applying them to the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{1}
appearing in (
VI.23
), we learn that the universes involved in the KT transformed theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
are

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1})_{e}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\text{ SSB}=\text{Trivial}_{0}\oplus\text{Trivial}_{1}

(VI.160)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1})_{m}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\text{ Trivial}=\text{Trivial}_{2}\,,

where
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
denotes the dual symmetry obtained after gauging the original
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{1}
. Thus in total,
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]
is an
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped phase with three vacua. The identification of the lines appearing in section
V.11
is

\begin{aligned} 1_{ee}&\equiv 1_{00}\oplus 1_{11},\\
P_{ee}&\equiv 1_{01}\oplus 1_{10},\end{aligned}\,\quad\begin{aligned} 1_{mm}&\equiv 1_{22}\\
P_{mm}&\equiv 1_{22}\end{aligned}\,\quad\begin{aligned} S_{em}&\equiv 1_{02}\oplus 1_{12},\\
S_{me}&\equiv 1_{20}\oplus 1_{21}\,.\end{aligned}

(VI.161)

This implies that the
\mathsf{Ising}
symmetry generators are identified as

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\equiv 1_{01}\oplus 1_{10}\oplus 1_{22}

(VI.162)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\equiv 1_{02}\oplus 1_{12}\oplus 1_{20}\oplus 1_{21}\,,

where we have used (
IV.175
).
Note that there are
non-trivial relative Euler terms
between the three vacua since the linking action (
V.116
) becomes

S_{em}:~v_{0}+v_{1}\to\sqrt{2}v_{2}\,\qquad S_{me}:~v_{2}\to\sqrt{2}(v_{0}+v_{1})\,,

(VI.163)

which means that the linking actions of the vacua changing lines are

\begin{aligned} 1_{02}&:~v_{0}\to v_{2}/\sqrt{2},\\
1_{12}&:~v_{1}\to v_{2}/\sqrt{2},\end{aligned}\,\qquad\begin{aligned} 1_{20}&:~v_{2}\to\sqrt{2}v_{0}\\
1_{21}&:~v_{2}\to\sqrt{2}v_{1}\,,\end{aligned}

(VI.164)

implying non-trivial relative Euler terms between vacua 0 and 2, and vacua 1 and 2, but trivial relative Euler terms between vacua 0 and 1. All these properties imply that
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]
is the
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase in which both
P
and
S
are spontaneously broken.

Applying the results of section
V.11
to the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{2}
appearing in (
VI.27
) leads to a similar analysis.

The
\mathsf{Ising}
-symmetric phase transition
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C}
obtained after applying the KT transformation to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetric Ising phase transition can be expressed as follows. It comprises of two universes

(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C})_{e}=\text{Ising}_{e}\,,\quad(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C})_{m}=\text{Ising}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\text{Ising}_{m}\,,

(VI.165)

where we have used the well-known isomorphism of
\text{Ising}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with Ising to express it as a copy
\text{Ising}_{m}
of Ising CFT. Note that there is no relative Euler term between the two Ising universes as the linking action of universe changing lines is

1_{em}:~\text{id}_{e}\to\text{id}_{m}\,\qquad 1_{me}:~\text{id}_{m}\to\text{id}_{e}

(VI.166)

on the identity local operators
\text{id}_{e}
and
\text{id}_{m}
of
\text{Ising}_{e}
and
\text{Ising}_{m}
respectively. These linking actions reproduce (
V.116
) for lines
S_{em}
and
S_{me}
as these lines can be expressed as

\displaystyle S_{em}

\displaystyle=S_{ee}\otimes 1_{em}=1_{em}\otimes S_{mm}

(VI.167)

\displaystyle S_{me}

\displaystyle=S_{mm}\otimes 1_{me}=1_{me}\otimes S_{ee}\,,

where
S_{ee}
and
S_{mm}
are Kramers-Wannier duality defects of
\text{Ising}_{e}
and
\text{Ising}_{m}
respectively, whose quantum dimensions are
\sqrt{2}
, i.e.

S_{ee}:~\text{id}_{e}\to\sqrt{2}\text{id}_{e}\,,\qquad S_{mm}:~\text{id}_{m}\to\sqrt{2}\text{id}_{m}\,.

(VI.168)

The schematic form of
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C}
is thus

\text{Ising}_{e}\oplus\text{Ising}_{m}
P
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C}\quad=
P
S

(VI.169)

The
\mathsf{Ising}
symmetry is realized as

P=\eta_{ee}\oplus\eta_{mm}\,,\quad S=S_{em}\oplus S_{me}\,,

(VI.170)

where
\eta_{ee}
and
\eta_{mm}
are the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetries of
\text{Ising}_{e}
and
\text{Ising}_{m}
respectively.

The relevant operator responsible for the transition is
\epsilon_{e}-\epsilon_{m}
. Adding this operator with positive sign sends
\text{Ising}_{e}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SSB phase and
\text{Ising}_{m}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
trivial phase, and hence we land on the
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase. On the other hand, adding this operator with negative sign sends
\text{Ising}_{e}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
trivial phase and
\text{Ising}_{m}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase, and hence we again land on the
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase.

The order parameters for the
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]
involves an operator with generalized charge
P\overline{P}
and two operators forming an
S\overline{S}
-multiplet of
\mathsf{Ising}
symmetry. Applying KT transformations (
V.120
) and (
V.121
) respectively to the identity operator
id
and the spin operator
\sigma
of the Ising CFT, we deduce that these order parameters are

{\mathcal{O}}_{P\overline{P}}=\text{id}_{e}-\text{id}_{m}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},u}=\sigma_{e}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},t}=\mu_{m}\,,

(VI.171)

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{P\overline{P}}
is the operator transforming in generalized charge
P\overline{P}
and the operators
\{{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},u},{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},t}\}
forming the
S\overline{S}
-multiplet. The IR images of above operators are

{\mathcal{O}}_{P\overline{P}}\equiv v_{0}+v_{1}-v_{2}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},u}\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},t}=v_{2}\,.

(VI.172)

The order parameters for the
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2}]
also involve an operator with generalized charge
P\overline{P}
and two operators forming an
S\overline{S}
-multiplet of
\mathsf{Ising}
symmetry. Applying KT transformations (
V.120
) and (
V.122
) respectively to the identity operator
id
and the disorder operator
\mu
of the Ising CFT, we deduce that these order parameters are

{\mathcal{O}}_{P\overline{P}}=\text{id}_{e}-\text{id}_{m}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(m)}_{S\overline{S},u}=\sigma_{m}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(m)}_{S\overline{S},t}=\mu_{e}\,,

(VI.173)

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{P\overline{P}}
is the operator transforming in generalized charge
P\overline{P}
and the operators
\{{\mathcal{O}}^{(m)}_{S\overline{S},u},{\mathcal{O}}^{(m)}_{S\overline{S},t}\}
forming the
S\overline{S}
-multiplet. The IR images of above operators are

{\mathcal{O}}_{P\overline{P}}\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}-v_{2}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},u}\equiv v_{1}-v_{2}\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}}^{(e)}_{S\overline{S},t}\equiv v_{0}\,.

(VI.174)

VI.12
Phase Transition between
(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
SSB and
(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
SSB Phases for
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
Symmetry

Now consider the KT transformation studied in section
V.12
. This KT transformation converts
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry to
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry. We use the Ising CFT to obtain a
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
-symmetric phase transition described below.

Using the map (
V.130
), we can quickly deduce that the
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
-symmetric gapped phases obtained after applying KT transformation on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SSB and trivial phases for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry are described respectively by the physical Lagrangian algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{1}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{+}+L_{0}^{-}+L_{2}^{+}+L_{2}^{-}+2L_{2,0}

(VI.175)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{+}+L_{0}^{-}+L_{1,0}+L_{2,0}+L_{3,0}\,,

which correspond to the following
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
-symmetric gapped phases respectively

\displaystyle=\text{$(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})$ SSB phase for $\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})$ symmetry}

(VI.176)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}}_{2}]

\displaystyle=\text{$(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})$ SSB phase for $\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})$ symmetry}\,.

A
(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{q})
SSB phase for
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{N})
symmetry with
pq=N
has
p+q
vacua, where the first
p
vacua comprise a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
SSB phase for the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
subsymmetry, and the last
q
vacua comprise a
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
SSB phase for the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
subsymmetry, with these
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
SSB phases exchanged by the action of non-invertible duality defect
S\in\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{N})
. For more details on these gapped phases, we refer the reader to
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
.

Another equivalent way to deduce these
\mathsf{TY}(Z_{4})
-symmetric gapped phases is to apply the results of section
V.12
. Applying them to the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{1}
appearing in (
VI.23
), we learn that the universes involved in the KT transformed theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
are

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1})^{e}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\text{ SSB}=\text{Trivial}_{0}\oplus\text{Trivial}_{1}

(VI.177)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1})^{m}_{1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\text{ Trivial}=\text{Trivial}_{2}

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1})^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\text{ Trivial}=\text{Trivial}_{3}

where
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
denotes the dual symmetry obtained after gauging the original
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{1}
. Thus in total,
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]
is a
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped phase with four vacua. This is enough to fix it to
(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
SSB phase. We can also identify the
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
lines following (
IV.195
) as

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\equiv 1_{01}\oplus 1_{10}\oplus 1_{23}\oplus 1_{32}

(VI.178)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\equiv 1_{02}\oplus 1_{12}\oplus 1_{03}\oplus 1_{13}\oplus 1_{20}\oplus 1_{21}\oplus 1_{30}\oplus 1_{31}\,,

which is precisely the realization of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry in the
(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
SSB phase. Note that (
IV.199
) is satisfied by linking action

1_{02}:~v_{0}\to v_{2}\,,

(VI.179)

which implies that there are no relative Euler terms between any of the four vacua, reproducing again a property expected of the
(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
SSB phase as described in
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
.

Applying the results of section
V.12
to the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{2}
appearing in (
VI.27
), we learn that the universes involved in the KT transformed theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
are

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2})^{e}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\text{ Trivial}=\text{Trivial}_{0}

(VI.180)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2})^{m}_{1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\text{ SSB}=\text{Trivial}_{1}\oplus\text{Trivial}_{2}

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2})^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\text{ SSB}=\text{Trivial}_{3}\oplus\text{Trivial}_{4}

where
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
denotes the dual symmetry obtained after gauging the original
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of
\mathfrak{T}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}_{2}
. Thus in total,
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2}]
is a
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
-symmetric (1+1)d gapped phase with five vacua. This is enough to fix it to
(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
SSB phase. We can also identify the
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
lines following (
IV.195
) as

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\equiv 1_{00}\oplus 1_{13}\oplus 1_{24}\oplus 1_{32}\oplus 1_{41}

(VI.181)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\equiv 1_{01}\oplus 1_{02}\oplus 1_{03}\oplus 1_{04}\oplus 1_{10}\oplus 1_{20}\oplus 1_{30}\oplus 1_{40}\,,

which is precisely the realization of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry in the
(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
SSB phase. Note that (
IV.199
) is satisfied by linking action

1_{01}:~v_{0}\to 2v_{1}\,,

(VI.182)

which implies that there is a
non-trivial relative Euler term
between vacua 0 and
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
but trivial relative Euler terms between vacua
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, reproducing again a property expected of the
(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
SSB phase as described in
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
.

The
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
-symmetric phase transition
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C}
obtained after applying the KT transformation to the Ising phase transition can be expressed as follows. It comprises of three universes

(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C})^{e}_{0}=\text{Ising}^{e}_{0}\,,\quad\begin{aligned} (\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C})^{m}_{1}&=(\text{Ising}/\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{\sqrt{2}}=(\text{Ising}^{m}_{1})_{\sqrt{2}}\\
(\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C})^{m}_{2}&=(\text{Ising}/\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{\sqrt{2}}=(\text{Ising}^{m}_{2})_{\sqrt{2}}\,.\end{aligned}

(VI.183)

The schematic form of
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C}
is thus

\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{C}=\text{Ising}^{e}_{0}\oplus\text{Ising}^{m}_{1}\oplus\text{Ising}^{m}_{2}
S
A
A

(VI.184)

The
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry is realized as

A=\eta_{00}\oplus 1_{12}\oplus\eta_{21}\,,\quad S=S_{01}\oplus S_{02}\oplus S_{10}\oplus S_{20}\,.

(VI.185)

The relevant operator responsible for the transition is
\epsilon^{e}_{0}-\epsilon^{m}_{1}-\epsilon^{m}_{2}
. Adding this operator with positive sign sends
\text{Ising}^{e}_{0}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SSB phase and
\text{Ising}^{m}_{i}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
trivial phase, and hence we land on the
(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
SSB phase. On the other hand, adding this operator with negative sign sends
\text{Ising}^{e}_{0}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
trivial phase and
\text{Ising}^{m}_{i}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase, and hence we land on the
(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
SSB phase.

The order parameters for the
(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
SSB phase
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]
involve a copy each of
L_{0}^{-},L_{2}^{\pm}
multiplets and two distinct
L_{2,0}
multiplets of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry. Applying KT transformations (
V.132
) and (
V.134
) respectively to the identity operator
id
and the spin operator
\sigma
of the Ising CFT, we deduce that these order parameters are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{-}

\displaystyle=\text{id}^{e}_{0}-\text{id}^{m}_{1}-\text{id}^{m}_{2}

(VI.186)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)}

\displaystyle=\text{id}^{m}_{1}-\text{id}^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}

\displaystyle=\text{id}^{e}_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{e}_{0}+\mu^{m}_{1}-\mu^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{e}_{0}-\mu^{m}_{1}+\mu^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{e}_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}

\displaystyle=\mu^{m}_{1}+\mu^{m}_{2}\,,

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{e}^{\pm}
form
L_{e}^{\pm}
multiplets and
\{{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(k)},{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(k)}\}
for
k=1,2
form the two
L_{2,0}
-multiplets of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry. The IR images of above operators are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{-}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}+v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}

(VI.187)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{2}-v_{3}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}+v_{1}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}+v_{2}-v_{3}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}-v_{2}+v_{3}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{2}+v_{3}\,.

The order parameters for the
(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
SSB phase
[\mathfrak{T}^{\mathcal{S}}_{2}]
involve a copy each of
L_{0}^{-},L_{1,0},L_{2,0},L_{3,0}
multiplets of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry. Applying KT transformations (
V.132
) and (
V.136
) respectively to the identity operator
id
and the disorder operator
\mu
of the Ising CFT, we deduce that these order parameters are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{-}

\displaystyle=\text{id}^{e}_{0}-\text{id}^{m}_{1}-\text{id}^{m}_{2}

(VI.188)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)}

\displaystyle=\text{id}^{m}_{1}-\text{id}^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}

\displaystyle=\text{id}^{e}_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{1,0}^{u}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{m}_{1}-i\sigma^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{1,0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mu^{e}_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{3,0}^{u}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{m}_{1}+i\sigma^{m}_{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{3,0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mu^{e}_{0}\,,

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{-}
forms
L_{0}^{-}
multiplet,
\{{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)},{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}\}
forms
L_{2,0}
-multiplet and
\{{\mathcal{O}}_{e,0}^{u},{\mathcal{O}}_{e,0}^{t}\}
form
L_{e,0}
-multiplets for
e=1,3
of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
symmetry. The IR images of above operators are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{-}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}-v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}

(VI.189)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{u,(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{1}+v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2,0}^{t,(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{1,0}^{u}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{1}-v_{2}-iv_{3}+iv_{4}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{1,0}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{3,0}^{u}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{1}-v_{2}+iv_{3}-iv_{4}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{3,0}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv v_{0}\,.

VI.13
Extensions: Higher-Order Phase Transitions and Multi-Critical Points

We can also apply KT transformations to known higher-order phase transitions and multi-critical points to obtain new higher-order phase transitions and multi-critical points, including the ones involving non-invertible symmetries.
For example, it is well-known that the tri-critical Ising CFT admits a relevant deformation preserving the
\mathsf{Ising}
symmetry that leads to the gapless Ising CFT on one side of the deformation and to the gapped
\mathsf{Ising}
SSB phase on the other side of the deformation. See e.g.
Chang et al. 2019
for more details.
We can now apply KT transformations to the tricritical Ising CFT with
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Ising}
, and obtain
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric CFTs (for some larger symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
) that act as phase transitions between
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapless phases on one side, and
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric gapped phases on the other side.

Such considerations are natural extensions of this paper, which we leave to future works.

URF\R1\231467. The work of SSN is supported by the UKRI Frontier Research Grant, underwriting the ERC Advanced Grant ”Generalized Symmetries in Quantum Field Theory and Quantum Gravity” and the Simons Foundation Collaboration on “Special Holonomy in Geometry, Analysis, and Physics”, Award ID: 724073, Schafer-Nameki.

Appendix A
Gapped (Boundary) Phases as Pivotal Higher-Functors

A.1
General Systems with Non-Invertible Symmetries

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a pivotal
10
10

10

In the cases of interest in this paper, we want the pivotal structure to be spherical (and in fact unitary).
fusion
(d-1)
-category characterizing symmetries of
d
-dimensional systems. Consider a
d
-dimensional system
\mathfrak{T}
, which may be a bulk or boundary system, or even a defect inside a higher-dimensional system. The
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry is realized on
\mathfrak{T}
by specifying a pivotal tensor
(d-1)
-functor

\phi:~{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{d-1}(\mathfrak{T})\,,

(A.1)

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{d-1}(\mathfrak{T})
is the pivotal monoidal
(d-1)
-category formed by topological defects of the system
\mathfrak{T}
. Different functors provide different realizations of the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathfrak{T}
. This includes different couplings to background fields for
{\mathcal{S}}
symmetry, including discrete torsion. The non-existence of such a functor means that
\mathfrak{T}
cannot be made
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric.

The system
\mathfrak{T}
may naturally lie in a family
\mathcal{F}
of
d
-dimensional systems satisfying certain desired properties. Then, we have a pivotal
d
-category
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}(\mathcal{F})
associated to
\mathcal{F}
in which objects are the systems in the family and morphisms are topological defects that may transition between different systems inside the family. We may now be interested in possible ways of realizing the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
on some system in the family
\mathcal{F}
. Such ways are classified by pivotal
d
-functors

\Phi:~B{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{d}(\mathcal{F})\,,

(A.2)

where
B{\mathcal{S}}
is the pivotal
d
-category obtained by delooping the pivotal fusion
(d-1)
-category
{\mathcal{S}}

11
11

11

The
d
-category
B{\mathcal{S}}
contains a single object, and the endomorphism
(d-1)
-category of this object is the pivotal fusion
(d-1)
-category
{\mathcal{S}}
.
. The image under
\Phi
of the sole object in
B{\mathcal{S}}
is the system
\mathfrak{T}\in\mathcal{F}
on which the symmetry
{\mathcal{S}}
is being realized. The rest of the information of
\Phi
descends to a pivotal tensor
(d-1)
-functor

\phi:~{\mathcal{S}}\to\text{End}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{d}(\mathcal{F})}(\mathfrak{T})\,,

(A.3)

where
\text{End}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{d}(\mathcal{F})}(\mathfrak{T})
is the pivotal monoidal
(d-1)
-category formed by endomorphisms of the object
\mathfrak{T}
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}(\mathcal{F})
, which are precisely the topological defects living in the system
\mathfrak{T}
, and hence we have
\text{End}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{d}(\mathcal{F})}(\mathfrak{T})={\mathcal{C}}_{d-1}(\mathfrak{T})
, recovering the description (
A.1
).

See also the recent review
Carqueville et al. 2023
which also takes this perspective on
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric systems.

A.2
Gapped Phases with Non-Invertible Symmetries

Taking
\mathcal{F}
to be the family
\mathcal{F}^{\text{top}}
formed by
d
-dimensional (unitary, oriented, fully extended) TQFTs leads to the classification of
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional TQFTs as the classification of pivotal
d
-functors

\Phi:~B{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{d}\left(\mathcal{F}^{\text{top}}\right)\,.

(A.4)

The non-symmetric
d
-dimensional TQFT underlying an
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional TQFT is obtained as the image under
\Phi
of the sole object of
B{\mathcal{S}}
. The
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric
d
-dimensional gapped phases are then obtained as deformation classes of such
d
-functors.

In the special case
d=2
, the irreducible 2d TQFTs are
\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda}
parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. There is a single irreducible interface
{\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}
from
\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda_{1}}
to
\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda_{2}}
whose linking action on the identity operator
1_{\lambda_{1}}
of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda_{1}}
is

{\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}:~1_{\lambda_{1}}\to e^{-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})}1_{\lambda_{2}}\,,

(A.5)

where
1_{\lambda_{2}}
is the identity operator of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda_{2}}
. The corresponding pivotal 2-category is denoted as

{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\left(\mathcal{F}^{\text{top}}\right)=2\text{-}\mathsf{Vec}^{\odot}

(A.6)

and understood as the Euler completion of the 2-category of 2-vector spaces. See
Carqueville et al. 2019
for more details on the operation of Euler completion. Thus,
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric (where
{\mathcal{S}}
is a fusion category) (1+1)d gapped phases are obtained as deformation classes of pivotal 2-functors of the form

\Phi:~B{\mathcal{S}}\to 2\text{-}\mathsf{Vec}^{\odot}\,.

(A.7)

The complete information of such a 2-functor
\Phi
can be extracted from the SymTFT sandwich construction, as discussed in detail in
Bhardwaj et al. 2025
.

As a special case of the above, consider the case when the underlying 2d TQFT is trivial, i.e. the image of
\Phi
picks out the object
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
in
2\text{-}\mathsf{Vec}^{\odot}
. In this case, we have

\text{End}_{2\text{-}\mathsf{Vec}^{\odot}}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0})=\mathsf{Vec}\,,

(A.8)

where
\mathsf{Vec}
denotes the category formed by finite dimensional vector spaces. The possible
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric TQFTs whose underlying TQFT is trivial are known as symmetry protected topological (SPT) phases for
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetry. Applying (
A.1
), we recover the well-known fact
Thorngren and Wang 2024
that 2d SPT phases for
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetry are classified by fiber functors from
{\mathcal{S}}
, i.e. functors of the form

\phi:~{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathsf{Vec}\,.

(A.9)

In fact, by what we discussed above, we also have

\text{End}_{2\text{-}\mathsf{Vec}^{\odot}}(\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda})=\mathsf{Vec}

(A.10)

for any
\lambda
. Thus, we learn that any
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric 2d TQFT whose underlying non-symmetric 2d TQFT is invertible can be obtained by stacking the underlying invertible 2d TQFT with an SPT phase.

A.3
Gapped Boundary Phases with Non-Invertible Symmetries

As another instance relevant for this paper, take
\mathcal{F}
to be the family
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}}^{\text{top}}
formed by all topological boundary conditions of a
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
. Then
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}}^{\text{top}}\right)
is the pivotal
d
-category
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1})
formed by topological boundary conditions of the TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
.
{\mathcal{S}}
-symmetric topological boundary conditions of the TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
are then classified by pivotal
d
-functors

\Phi:~B{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1})\,.

(A.11)

Moving forward, let’s restrict to
d=2
and
\mathfrak{Z}_{d+1}
to be an irreducible 3d TQFT
\mathfrak{Z}
admitting gapped boundary conditions. As discussed in the main text, such a 3d TQFT can be identified as the SymTFT associated to a unitary fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}

\mathfrak{Z}\cong\mathfrak{Z}({\mathcal{C}})\,.

(A.12)

Note that in general there are multiple choices of
{\mathcal{C}}
satisfying the above relation, all of which are related by Morita equivalences.

The 2-category
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{Z})
is completely determined in terms of
{\mathcal{C}}
, whose Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
is equivalent to the MTC
\mathcal{Z}

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})\cong\mathcal{Z}\,.

(A.13)

Let us describe the information of
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{Z})
in terms of the information of
{\mathcal{C}}
. The simple objects of
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{Z})
are irreducible topological boundary conditions of
\mathfrak{Z}
and they are parametrized by pairs
({\mathcal{M}},\lambda)
where
{\mathcal{M}}
is an indecomposable module category for
{\mathcal{C}}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. We label the corresponding irreducible topological boundary condition, or the corresponding simple object, as
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{M}})
.

The morphisms

\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{M}}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\big)

(A.14)

from
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{M}})
to
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
are the given by the category
{\mathcal{N}}
having the property

{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes_{{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\,,

(A.15)

where
\boxtimes_{{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}}
is the Deligne product taken relative to the fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}
obtained as the dual of
{\mathcal{C}}
with respect to
{\mathcal{M}}
. The category
{\mathcal{N}}
describes the topological lines acting as interfaces from
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{M}})
to
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
. In particular, we have

\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{M}}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}})\big)={\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}

(A.16)

and

\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{C}}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}({\mathcal{C}})\big)={\mathcal{C}}

(A.17)

for the special case of a regular module
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{C}}
. Thus, the topological line operators on a boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{M}})
form the fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}
, and for the special case
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{C}}
, we have that the topological line operators on
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{C}})
form the fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Let us now describe some properties of the pivotal structure. Consider a simple line operator
L
in
{\mathcal{N}}=\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{1}}({\mathcal{M}}_{1}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}_{2})\big)
. It has some quantum dimension
d_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(L)
, i.e. its linking action on the identity operator
1_{\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{1}}({\mathcal{M}}_{1})}
of the boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{1}}({\mathcal{M}}_{1})
is

L:~1_{\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{1}}({\mathcal{M}}_{1})}\to d_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(L)1_{\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}_{2})}\,,

(A.18)

where
1_{\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}_{2})}
is the identity operator on the boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}_{2})
. Then the quantum dimension of the same operator
L
in
{\mathcal{N}}=\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}({\mathcal{M}}_{1}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}_{2})\big)
is

d_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}(L)=\frac{e^{-(\lambda^{\prime}_{2}-\lambda_{2})}}{e^{-(\lambda^{\prime}_{1}-\lambda_{1})}}d_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(L)\,.

(A.19)

Toric Code Example.

As an example, take
\mathfrak{Z}
to be the Toric Code. In this case, the only choice for
{\mathcal{C}}
is

{\mathcal{C}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\,.

(A.20)

There are two module categories
{\mathcal{M}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}=\mathsf{Vec}
. We thus have irreducible topological boundaries

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}),\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{Vec})

(A.21)

with

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\{1^{e}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},~P^{e}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\}

(A.22)

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{Vec}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathsf{Vec})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\{1^{m}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},~P^{m}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathsf{Vec})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Vec}=\{S^{em}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{Vec}),\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Vec}=\{S^{me}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\}\,,

where we have displayed the simple lines as well.

The non-zero fusion rules are

\displaystyle 1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes 1^{i}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle=1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}

(A.23)

\displaystyle 1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes P^{i}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}=P^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes 1^{i}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle=P^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle P^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes P^{i}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle=1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle 1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes S^{ij}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}=S^{ij}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes 1^{j}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle=S^{ij}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle P^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes S^{ij}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}=S^{ij}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes P^{j}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle=S^{ij}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle S^{ij}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\otimes S^{ji}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}

\displaystyle=1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}\oplus P^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}\,,

where
i,j\in\{e,m\}
, and the quantum dimensions are

\displaystyle d\left(1^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})}

(A.24)

\displaystyle d\left(P^{i}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})}

\displaystyle d\left(S^{ij}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,e^{-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})}

Appendix B
Condensable Algebras

The conditions that condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=\oplus_{a}n^{a}a
in
\mathcal{Z}
must satisfy are
Chatterjee and Wen 2023

(CA1)

n^{\mathbf{1}}=1,\quad n^{a}\in\mathbb{N},\quad n^{a}=n^{\overline{a}}

(CA2)

s_{a}=0\text{ for }a\in\mathcal{A}

(CA3)

\frac{\sum_{b\in\mathcal{Z}}S^{ab}n^{b}}{\sum_{b\in\mathcal{Z}}S^{1b}n^{b}}=\text{ cyclotomic integer for all }a\in\mathcal{Z}

(CA4)

n^{a}\leq d_{a}-\delta\left(d_{a}\right)

(CA5)

n^{a}n^{b}\leq\sum_{c}N_{c}^{ab}n^{c}-\delta_{a,\overline{b}}\delta\left(d_{a}\right)

(CA6)

n^{a}=\sum_{b}S^{ab}n^{b}\text{ if }\mathcal{A}\text{ is Lagrangian. }

Here
S
is the S-matrix and
s_{a}
the spin, and
\delta(d_{a})
is defined as

\begin{cases}\delta(d_{a})=0\text{ if }d_{a}\in\mathbb{N}\\
\delta(d_{a})=1\text{ if }d_{a}\notin\mathbb{N}\end{cases}

(B.1)

We note these are only the necessary conditions ( see the Introduction – around (
I.6
) – for further discussion).
We proceed by identifying algebras that satisfy the above necessary conditions and then check that there is a consistent condensation, assuming the limitations discussed above. Once we have a condensable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, the associated reduced topological order
\mathcal{Z}/{\mathcal{A}}
is determined by computing local
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})

Kong 2014
, which form a “smaller” modular tensor category
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
.
